L6: #### ABSTRACT
L7:
L8:
L9: Generalizing and unifying prior results, we solve the subconvexity problem for the L-functions of GL1
L10:
L11:
L12: |  | and GL2 automorphic representations over a fixed number field, uniformly in all aspects. A novel feature of the |  |
L13:
L14: | --- | --- | --- |
L15:
L16: |  | present method is the softness of our arguments; this is largely due to a consistent use of canonically normalized |  |
L17:
L18: |  | period relations, such as those supplied by the work of Waldspurger and Ichino–Ikeda. |  |

L102:
L103:
L104: ## introduction, in explicit terms, to some of the ideas of this paper.

L115: #### 2 PHILIPPE MICHEL, AKSHAY VENKATESH
L116:
L117:
L118: Throughout this paper, F denotes a fixed number field and A its ring
L119:
L120:
L121: of ad\`eles. For π an automorphic representation of GLn(A) (with unitary central character, not necessarily of finite order), Iwaniec and Sarnak have attached
L122:
L123:
L124: \[
L125:
L126: an analytic conductor C(\pi) \in R>1; it is the product of the usual
L127:
L128: \]
L129:
L130:
L131: (integer) conductor with a parameter measuring how large the archimedean eigenvalues are. More intrinsically, the logarithm of the conductor is proportional to 
L132: the density of zeros of the corresponding L-function. See §4.1.
L133:
L134: The subconvexity problem is concerned with the size of L(π, s) when
L135:
L136:
L137: \[
L138:
L139: <s = \frac{1}{2}: it consists in improving over the so-called convexity bound (see [37] for instance) L(\pi,1/2) n,F,\epsilon C(\pi) 1/4+\epsilon for any1 
L140: \epsilon > 0. The main result of the present paper is the resolution of this problem for GL1 and GL2-automorphic representations: 1.1. Theorem. — There is an 
L141: absolute constant \delta > 0 such that: for
L142:
L143: \]
L144:
L145:
L146: π an automorphic representation of GL1(A) or GL2(A) (with unitary central character), one has
L147:
L148:
L149: L(π, 1/2) F C(π) 1/4−δ
L150:
L151:
L152: .
L153:
L154:
L155: Remark. — Contrary to appearance, this also includes the question of
L156:
L157:
L158: growth along the critical line, i.e. what is called the t-aspect, because
L159:
L160:
L161: \[
L162:
L163: L(\pi,1/2 + it) = L(\pi \otimes\|.\| it
L164:
L165: \]
L166:
L167:
L168: A, 1/2).
L169:
L170:
L171: For example, an interesting corollary is a subconvex bound for the L-function
L172:
L173:
L174: 2 at the point_t = 1/2 + iν. Another of a Maass form with eigenvalue_1/4 + ν corollary is a subconvex bound (in the discriminant) for the Dedekind Lfunction of 
L175: a cubic extension of F (cf. \[21\] for an application of the latter). The above result is a specialization (by taking \pi_2 to be a suitable Eisenstein series) 
L176: of the following more general result 1.2. Theorem. — There is an absolute2 constant δ > 0 such that: for \pi_1, \pi_2 automorphic representations on GL2(AF ) we
L177:  have3 : L(π1 \otimes \pi_2, 1/2) F,π2 C(π1 \otimes \pi_2) 1/4−δ ; (1.1) more precisely, the constant implied depends polynomially on the discriminant of F (for
L178:  F varying over fields of given degree) and on C(π2).
L179:
L180:
L181: 1 Recently, Heath-Brown has established a general convexity bound \[30\], which together with the work
L182:
L183:
L184: 1/4
L185:
L186:
L187: of Luo, Rudnick, Sarnak \[46\] implies the clean convexity bound L(π, 1/2) n,F C(π)
L188:
L189:
L190: .
L191:
L192:
L193: 2 independent of the number field F.
L194:
L195: 3 More precisely we prove the bound L(π1 \otimes \pi_2, 1/2) F,π2 C(π1) 1/2−2δ
L196:
L197:
L198: . That the latter implies the
L199:
L200:
L201: former is a consequence of the bounds in \[10\].
L202:
L203:
L204: #### THE SUBCONVEXITY PROBLEM FOR GL2. 3
L205:
L206:
L207: The value of δ is easily computable. We have not attempted to optimize
L208:
L209:
L210: any exponent, our goal in this paper being of giving clean proofs in a general context.
L211:
L212:
L213: Remark. — The bound generalizes (up to the value of δ) a variety of subconvex bounds [71,12,29,27,47,17,18,64,22,34,19,42,44,48,6,54,31,9,8, 67,16]. Its main 
L214: feature, however, is its uniformity in the various possible parameters (the so-called “conductor”, t-aspect, or “spectral” aspects): such bounds are sometimes 
L215: called “hybrid”. The first such hybrid bound is that of Heath-Brown \[29\] for Dirichlet character L-functions; recent hybrid bounds are to be found in the work
L216:  of Jutila and Motohashi \[54\] and in the work of Blomer and Harcos \[8\]. The present work generalizes these also, again, up to the value of δ.
L217:
L218:
L219: However, in saying this we have done an injustice to some of those papers; in some cases, the very point was to obtain the best exponent, whereas
L220:
L221:
L222: our emphasis is quite different. For example, let us compare the present result to that of \[54\]. This paper gives, in particular, the uniform bound for
L223:
L224:
L225: the value L(ϕλ, 1 1/3 where ϕλ is a Maass form. This bound 2 + it) (\|t\| + \|λ\|) is very strong – the same exponent as the classical Weyl bound; on the 
L226: other hand, it fails to be subconvex when_t ∼ λ, where the conductor drops. The present work fills this lacuna and provides a subconvex bound for that “critical 
L227: point”; as far as we are aware this subconvex bound is new even over Q. On the other hand, while our method presumably leads to a respectable δ, it would not be
L228:  so strong as the result of \[54\], one reason being that we are using the amplification method. It is also worth observing that the phenomena of the conductor 
L229: dropping often leads to major difficulties, both in
L230:
L231:
L232: our methods and other treatments.
L233:
L234:
L235: Remark. — It is reasonable to ask what one hopes by studying hybrid
L236:
L237:
L238: settings – especially given that many applications of subconvexity do not require them.
L239:
L240:
L241: It is generally believed that the analytic behavior of L-functions are
L242:
L243:
L244: \[
L245:
L246: =universal,” in that they are controlled by a single scaling parameter, the analytic conductor C. Taking C \to \infty in different ways can correspond to 
L247: analysis of eigenfunctions of large eigenvalue; analysis of eigenfunctions on a surface of large volume; or sections of a highly ample holomorphic bundle, and
L248: the reasons why these should all have similar asymptotic behavior is not clear.
L249:
L250: \]
L251:
L252:
L253: We may hope to achieve some insight into this “universality” by studying hybrid phenomena. Indeed, at many points in the text, the reader will
L254:
L255:
L256: #### 4 PHILIPPE MICHEL, AKSHAY VENKATESH
L257:
L258:
L259: note the close parallel between increasing the level at finite primes, and increasing the eigenvalue at archimedean primes.
L260:
L261:
L262: Our methods were outlined in our ICM announcement \[50\]. In particular
L263:
L264:
L265: we do not use trace formulas of any kind. An additional feature (originating in \[48\]), is that a special case of Theorem_1.2 (the case of \pi_1 a character) 
L266: enters the proof of the full theorem. That special case is proven in Theorem_5.1, and is based (following \[67\]) on a study of the equidistribution of cycles 
L267: on adelic quotients.
L268:
L269:
L270: We have also tried to make use of the following ideas to simplify the
L271:
L272:
L273: proof (both notationally and conceptually):
L274:
L275:
L276: - Sobolev norms (cf. \[4\]; in the adelic context \[67\]); 
L277:
L278: - Canonically normalized period formulas (see \[69,33\]); 
L279:
L280: - Regularization of integrals of automorphic forms (we give a self-contained treatment that avoids truncation).
L281:
L282: There remain many interesting questions related to the subconvexity
L283:
L284:
L285: story even for GL2. For instance, the “approximate functional equation” gives a way to numerically compute any given L-function at the central point, in
L286:
L287:
L288: time C_1/2+ε
L289:
L290:
L291: , where C is the analytic conductor. An interesting question is
L292:
L293:
L294: whether some of the ideas that enter into the proof of subconvexity can be interpreted to give faster algorithms. There is some suggestion of this in existing 
L295: fast algorithms for computation of ζ(1/2 + it).
L296:
L297:
L298: 1.1.1. An outline of the proof. — To conclude this section we outline
L299:
L300:
L301: the proof of the main theorem_1.2; a more elementary discussion is in §1.2 and §1.4. Consider two (generic) automorphic representations \pi_1, \pi_2; for 
L302: simplicity we assume that both are cuspidal4 and that \pi_2 is fixed. We aim for
L303:
L304:
L305: a subconvex bound for the Rankin/Selberg L-function central value of the form
L306:
L307:
L308: \[
L309:
L310: L(\pi 1 \otimes \pi_2,1/2) -\delta C(\pi 1 \otimes \pi_2) \frac{1}{4} \pi_2 C(\pi 1), \delta > 0. By Rankin/Selberg theory we may realize the left-hand side 
L311: above as a triple product period R \varphi 1\varphi 2E(g)dg = h\varphi 1, \varphi_2 E_i for suitable

L322: \pi_1 and \pi 2.Now\|h\varphi 1, \varphi 2Ei\|2 is bounded by h\varphi 2E, \varphi_2 E_i = h\varphi 2\varphi 2, EEi, \tag{1.2}

L350:  EEi = hϕ2ϕ2, EEireg) and Φ is a certain non-unitary Eisenstein series, which is an artifact of the integral regularization; on the other hand, π varies over
L351: the automorphic dual of PGL2, dµP is a “Plancherel measure,” and ϕ varies over an orthonormal basis, B(π), of factorizable vectors in π. Now: 1. The term hϕ2ϕ2,

L353: implies that hϕ, EEireg factor into a product of local integrals to which bounds for matrix coefficient can be applied (see below) times the central value L(π,
L354:  1/2)L(π × χ, 1/2); we eventually need only bounds for L(π × χ, 1/2), where π is essentially fixed5 and χ varying.
L355:
L356: The phenomenon of reduction to another case of subconvexity was noted
L357:
L358:
L359: by the first-named author in \[48\]. We establish this case in Theorem 5.1 (again, in all aspects); the proof generalizes \[67\], and we refer to the
L360: introduction of \[67\] and to §1.2 for intuition about it.
L361:
L362:
L363: 1.1.2. Local computations. — Let us be more precise about the local integrals that occur in, e.g., the Rankin-Selberg method. For our purpose
L364:
L365:
L366: they must be examined carefully; we are particularly interested in their analytic properties, i.e., how large or small they can be. This is the purpose of
L367:
L368:
L369: Part III of the paper; we deal also with the local Hecke integrals.
L370:
L371:
L372: For example, the local integral occuring in the Rankin-Selberg method
L373:
L374:
L375: can be interpreted as a linear functional \` on the tensor product \pi_1 \otimes \pi_2 \otimes \pi_3 of three representations \pi_j of GL2(k). The space of such
L376:  functionals is at
L377:
L378:
L379: 2 is therefore proportional to the Hermitian form

L400: quantities h\varphi 2\varphi 2, \varphi ireg decay very rapidly as the eigenvalue or level of \varphi increases. So, we may regard \varphi as essentially fixed.

L422:
L423:
L424: 1.1.3. Spectral identities of L-functions. — The identity (1.2), although
L425:
L426:
L427: evident, is the keystone of our argument. Its usage in the “period” form presented above seems to have been noticed independently by the present authors
L428:
L429:
L430: in their attempt to geometrize \[48\], and by Bernstein and Reznikov in
L431:
L432:
L433: their work on the subconvexity problem for triple product L-functions \[5\].
L434:
L435:
L436: However (1.2) also manifests itself at the level of L-functions, and looks
L437:
L438:
L439: \[
L440:
L441: rather striking in this guise: indeed \tag{1.3}
L442: \]
L443:
L444: \[
L445:
L446: may be recognized as an identity between (weighted) sums of central values of triple product L-functions and (weighted) sums of =canonical” square roots of
L447: similar central values: Z w(\pi 1)L(\pi 1 \otimes \pi_2 \otimes \pi_3,1/2)d\mu P (\pi 1) \pi_1
L448: \]
L449:
L450: \[
L451:
L452: Z
L453:
L454:
L455: \[
L456:
L457: p L(\pi 2 \otimes \pi \tilde{2} \otimes \pi,1/2). p L(\pi 3 \otimes \pi \tilde{3} \otimes \pi,1/2)d\mu P (\pi) = \tilde{w}(\pi)
L458: \]
L459:
L460:
L461: \]
L462:
L463: π
L464:
L465:
L466: In this form, this identity was discovered already by N. Kuznetsov \[43\] (with \pi_2, \pi_3 Eisenstein series see also \[53\]) and an interesting application
L467: of this result was made by M. Jutila \[40\]. This period identity has – implicitly or explicitly – played an important role in the analytic theory of GL2 forms.
L468:
L469: We refer to §4.5 for some more discussion of how to convert (1.2) to an identity of L-functions.
L470:
L471:
L472: In fact, it was shown by A. Reznikov (in the archimedean setting at
L473:
L474:
L475: least), that such phenomena is not isolated, and may be systematically described through the formalism of strong Gelfand configurations: that is com
L476:
L477:
L478: mutative diagrams of algebraic groups
L479:
L480:
L481: G

L520:
L521: 1.1.4. Structure of the paper; reading suggestions. — The paper splits
L522:
L523:
L524: into five parts; the first four parts are largely independent of each other, and contain various results of independent interest. The fifth part brings together
L525:  these results to prove the main theorem.
L526:
L527:
L528: The reader may wish to skip directly to Parts IV and V of the paper; the results of Parts II and III are largely technical and not particularly
L529:
L530:
L531: surprising.
L532:
L533:
L534: - In the remainder of Part I, §1.2, §1.4 we consider two corollaries to our main theorem – which can both be phrased without L-functions – and we explain how
L535: the proofs work in these instances. Indeed, the general proof is obtained by adelizing and combining these two particular cases.
L536:
L537: The corollaries we consider are:
L538:
L539:
L540: 1. In §1.2, we discuss the “Burgess bound,” which relates to the issue of the smallest quadratic non-residue modulo a prime q.
L541:
L542:
L543: 2. In §1.4, we discuss a problem in analysis on a negatively curved
L544:
L545:
L546: |  | surface, viz.: how large can the Fourier coefficients of an eigenfunction | 
L547:
L548: | --- | --- | 
L549:
L550: |  | along a closed geodesic be ? | 
L551:
L552: |  | - Part II (viz. §2.2 to §2.6) is of more general nature: we discuss a | 
L553:
L554: | system of Sobolev norms on adelic quotients, inspired largely by work of |  | 
L555:
L556: | Bernstein and Reznikov. This section exists to give a suitable language |  | 
L557:
L558: | for talking about adelic equidistribution, and the norms are a somewhat |  | 
L559:
L560: | cleaner version of those appearing in [67]. Some of the remarks here are |  | 
L561:
L562: | of independent interest, although they are of technical nature. |  | 
L563:
L564: |  | - Part III discusses some of the analytic theory of torus-invariant functionals on a representation of GL2(k) (where k is a local field), and of | 
L565:
L566: | trilinear functionals on representations of GL2(k). |  | 
L567:
L568: |  | - Part IV discusses the global theory of torus periods on GL2 and the | 
L569:
L570: | diagonal period on GL2 × GL2 × GL2. |  | 
L571:
L572: |  | - Part V gives the proof of Theorem 1.2 along with the important intermediary result Theorem 5.1 (a subconvex bound of L-function of character | 
L573:
L574: | twist uniform in the character aspect). |  |

L819: 1.2.4. Equidistribution statements. — We shall try to establish (1.5) by first proving uniform distribution of a “bigger” set, and then refining that statement.
L820:  Consider, then, the following three equidistribution statements, as q → ∞: (A) The closed horocycle {Λx : x ∈ [0, q]} becomes uniformly distributed on X; (B)
L821: {Λx : x ∈ Z ∩ [0, q]} becomes uniformly distributed on X; (C) {Λx : x ∈ Z ∩ [0, q]} becomes u.d. on X, when each point Λx is weighted by χ(x). For χ the
L822: quadratic character modulo q, we might rewrite (C) as: (C2) {Λx : x ∈ Z ∩ [0, q], x a quadratic residue mod q} becomes u.d. on X.

L869:
L870: 1.3. Ergodic principle. — Suppose a group H acts ergodically on a probability space (X, µ), and ν is an H-invariant measure which is dominated by some positive
L871: multiple of µ. Then ν is a scalar multiple of µ. By “ν is dominated by some positive multiple of µ” (written ν µ) we mean that there is a constant A such that
L872: for any measurable set S ⊂ X, \|ν\|(S) 6 Aµ(S). Indeed, if this is so, ν is absolutely continuous with respect to µ, and thus may be expressed as fµ for some f
L873: ∈ L_1 (µ); then f must be a H-invariant function, necessarily constant by ergodicity. The principle is therefore trivial; on the other hand, its consequences in
L874:  the number-theoretic context are surprising.
L875:
L876:
L877: Using the principle, we can pass from the equidistribution of µ to the
L878:
L879:
L880: \[
L881:
L882: equidistribution of \nu . (Of course,one needs a more quantitative form of this principle; we enunciate such a form in the context we need in §2.5.3.) By
L883: applying the ergodic principle to the group {n(t) : t \in Z}, we establish the implication (A) =\Rightarrow (B). To show that (B) =\Rightarrow (C) is a little
L884:
L885: \]
L886:
L887:
L888: more subtle, because the choice of H is not clear; we will need to pass to a covering to uncover it! We now discuss this in more detail.

L1114: 1.4.2. Geodesic restriction problems in the arithmetic case. — As was
L1115:
L1116:
L1117: explained to us by A. Reznikov, a consequence to our main result, Theorem_1.2, is that this behavior never occurs on an surface which is of arithmetic type and
L1118: when ϕλ varies amongst a suitable orthogonal basis of Laplacian eigenfunctions. We give definitions of these concepts below. In such a situation, we obtain a
L1119: much stronger result: let {ϕλ} be a basis of Hecke-Laplace eigenfunctions. Then there is an absolute constant δ > 0 such that \|a(ϕλ, ω)\| 6 C(M, G )λ −δ
L1120: kϕλkL2(M) . (1.6) The bound on the right hand side is independent of ω, and includes the “difficult” case when ω and λ are close. Thus the Fourier coefficients
L1121: of an Laplace/Hecke eigenform along a fixed geodesic decay uniformly: such uniformity is a direct consequence of the hybrid nature of the subconvex bound
L1122:
L1123:
L1124: proven in Theorem 1.2.
L1125:
L1126: If M is an arithmetic hyperbolic surface, then it is expected that (1.6) holds for any orthonormal basis of Laplace eigenforms. This comes from the fact that,
L1127: in the hyperbolic case, the multiplicities of Laplace eigenvalues are expected to be small, so that any Laplace eigenfunction could be expressed as a short
L1128: linear combination of Laplace/Hecke eigenfunctions.

L1571:
L1572:
L1573: B of Vinogradov to mean: there exists a constant c so that A_6 cB. If we write A δ cB, it means that the constant c is permitted to depend on δ, and so on. We
L1574: shall also use a modification of this notation: A B? means that there exist constants c1, c2 so that A_6 c1Bc2
L1575:
L1576:
L1577: .
L1578:
L1579:
L1580: Although the notation A  B is generally understood to mean that
L1581:
L1582:
L1583: the implicit constant is absolute, it is extremely convenient in our context to allow it to depend on certain predetermined parameters (e.g., the number field
L1584: over which we work) without explicit comment. We gather together
L1585:
L1586:
L1587: at this point references to where these conventions are introduced, for the convenience of the reader. To wit: in Part II, constants may depend on the
L1588: isomorphism class of (G, ρ) over the number field F; in Part III, the constants may depend on the discriminant of the local field (§3.1.3); in Parts
L1589:
L1590:
L1591: IV and V, they may depend on the isomorphism class of the ground field F.

L1593:

L1752:
L1753: 2.1.6. We now fix a normalization of left-invariant Haar measures on
L1754:
L1755:
L1756: the various groups: the Fv and A-points of G, as well as any parabolic subgroup P ⊂ G, as well as of any Levi factor M ⊂ P; and finally, on Kv, for every v.
L1757: These measures should have the following properties:
L1758:
L1759:
L1760: 1. The measures on adelic points should be the product of local measures;
L1761:
L1762:
L1763: 2. The projection P(F)\P(A) → M(F)\M(A) corresponding to the decomposition P = MN is measure-preserving (for the left Haar measures!)
L1764:
L1765:
L1766: 3. For all v, the measure on Kv has mass 1. For nonarchimedean v, it is the restriction of the measure from G(Fv).
L1767:
L1768:
L1769: 4. The map P(F)\P(A)×K → G(F)\G(A) should be measure-preserving (i.e., the preimage of any set has the same measure as the set).
L1770:
L1771: It is not difficult to construct such measures; we shall make an explicit choice, in the case of G = GL2, in §3.1.5. It is worth observing that,
L1772:

L6345: 3.5. Bounds for trilinear functionals, I: soft methods

L6352: sections.
L6353:

L9166: 4.3. Regularization
L9167:
L9168:
L9169: In this section, we define a regularization process to define integrals of
L9170:
L9171:
L9172: non-necessarily decaying functions on XPGL2 = PGL2(F)\\PGL2(A). Such a regularization was given by Zagier \[72\]; for our purposes, an alternate way of defining
L9173:  it using convolution will be of use.
L9174:

L9176: 4.3.1. For motivational purposes, we consider first a toy example, namely,
L9177:
L9178:
L9179: the corresponding situation on R>0 (one could even consider the case of the integers). We shall regard R>0 as a multiplicative group in what follows. A finite
L9180: function on R>0 is one whose translates, under the action of
L9181:
L9182:

L9184:

L9190:

L9193: is: f is admissible if the span hτyfi of all multiplicative translates does not contain the trivial representation of R>0. Let V+ be the space of continuous
L9194: functions on R>0 of rapid decay as x → ∞ (i.e. \|f(x)\| N \|x\| −N for all N); let V− be the space of rapid decay as x → 0; and let V0 = V+ ∩ V−. Let V be the
L9195: space of all continuous functions f on R>0 so that there exists admissible finite functions f1, f2 so that f − f1 ∈ V+, f − f2 ∈ V−. Clearly V0 ⊂ V . The
L9196: following Lemma is well-known:

L11045:
L11046:
L11047: .) Nonetheless, this quantity –
L11048:
L11049:
L11050: while very small – is in fact substantially larger than the contribution of the first Maass form. It is quite possible that error in the numerical integration
L11051: is responsible for most of the difference.
L11052:
L11053:
L11054: 4.5.3. The Motohashi formula. — One may also take the period of
L11055:
L11056:
L11057: (4.36) over a split torus, i.e., integrate R Et1 (a(y))Et2 (a(y))d ×y; the integral does not converge, but can be regularized as in §4.3.1.
L11058:
L11059: y∈R+
L11060:
L11061:
L11062: The resulting formula relates, on the left-hand side, Z
L11063:
L11064:
L11065: \[
L11066:
L11067: \xi(1/2 + t_1 + \nu)\xi(1/2 - t_1 + \nu)\xi(1/2 + t_2 + \lambda - \nu)\xi(1/2 - t_2 + \lambda - \nu) \nu \in iR
L11068:
L11069: \]
L11070:
L11071:
L11072: to, on the right hand side,
L11073:
L11074:
L11075: X Λ(1/2 + t1 + t2, ϕ)Λ(1/2 + t1 − t2, ϕ)Λ(1/2 + λ, ϕ)
L11076:
L11077:
L11078: M
L11079:
L11080:
L11081: + Eisenstein and degenerate terms
L11082:
L11083:
L11084: This is an example of a formula of Motohashi \[52\]; it is perhaps most interesting to let t1, t2, λ = 0.
L11085:
L11086: The possibility of thus deriving the Motohashi formula was remarked in
L11087:
L11088:
L11089: \[50\] in §4.3.3 together with the remark that the divergence of the resulting
L11090:
L11091:
L11092: #### THE SUBCONVEXITY PROBLEM FOR GL2. 89
L11093:
L11094:
L11095: integrals would “cause considerable technical difficulty.” The method of using regularized integrals thus settles that issue in a satisfactory way. We would
L11096: like to remark that it is necessary to be wary of the following point: the regularized integral cannot be interchanged naively with the continuous integral over
L11097:  the Eisenstein series that occurs on the right of (4.36). In “commuting” the two, we obtain an extra factor of η(−1/2−λ,t1,t2) η(−1/2+λ,t1,t2) ξ(2+2λ) +
L11098: ξ(2−2λ) .
L11099:
L11100:
L11101: 4.5.4. Commentary. — For the purpose of analytic number theory the
L11102:
L11103:
L11104: | above formulas, although beautiful, are insufficient; one needs a family with |  |
L11105: | --- | --- |
L11106: | --- | --- |
L11107: | more flexibility of “test functions.” This extra flexibility is provided by the |  |
L11108: | work of Motohashi and Kuznetsov. From the point of view of the method |  |
L11109: | above, this can be obtained by varying the choice of test vector in the representations underlying E |  |
L11110: | ∗ and E | ∗ |
L11111: | t1 | t2 . |
L11112:
L11113:
L11114: The associativity formula, and that of Motohashi, have played an important – though not always explicit – role in analytic number theory on
L11115:
L11116:
L11117: GL2. We invite the reader to see “shadows” of these formulas hiding in various other papers on the subject.
L11118:
L11119:
L11120: The primary advantage of the formalism above seems to be that it
L11121:
L11122:
L11123: generalizes immediately to ramified settings. One may, for instance, replace E ∗ by an Eisenstein series in the representation \chi_1 to get a new formula;
L11124:
L11125:
L11126: t1
L11127:
L11128:
L11129:
L11130: or one may replace Q by a number field. It is a very interesting question to investigate more deeply the “test function” version of such formulas in a general
L11131: setting, and to study the integral transforms – both archimedean and p-adic – that intervene.
L11132:
L11133:
L11134: Such general formulas would probably lead to, among other things, a
L11135:
L11136:
L11137: good exponent in the subconvexity theorem. In the present paper we have taken a “short cut” to subconvexity.
L11138:
L11139:
